@@7422346 <h> ITER Fusion Project : World Leader in Renewable Energy <p> Today 80% of the developed world 's energy comes from fossil which meets energy demand but these fuels present environmental issues and climate change challenges . <p> By 2050 , it is predicted that the population will be 9 billion and there will be a dramatic increase in energy consumption for which no one solution will be able to fill its demand.A new energy mix will prevail offering new and sustainable solutions for energy security , sustainable development and environmental protection . Future energy supply options may comprise of a new energy mix including fossil fuels , nuclear fission , fusion , and other renewable energy sources . <h> The Answers Are Found In Nature <p> Believing that the answers are found in nature , fusion may prove that theory correct once again . Fusion is the dominant source of energy for stars in the universe as well as on earth.It 's speculated that fusion energy could be the basis of energy which may some day take us to the stars in space travel.Nuclear -- means the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ entities merging together to form one single entity . <p> The sun is a powerful force . It produces energy through a process called fusion . The sun is made of atoms and it 's incredible gravity force pulls atoms together . These suns atoms or " light atoms " come together they " fuse " to make an entirely new heavier atom . That 's fusion . <p> High temperatures and pressures are required to force a fusion reaction to take place . Temperatures must reach approximately six times that found in the core of the sun . At this extreme heat , the hydrogen atom is no longer a gas but a plasma . It is a high-energy state of matter where electrons are stripped from their atoms . <p> Fusion differs from fission , which splits atoms and results in substantial radioactive waste , which is hazardous . <h> Fusion The Renewable Energy Source <p> At the International Atomic Energy Agency , Scientists have been studying the sun 's energy in the hopes of replicating it 's fusion capacity.Through technological innovation the goal has been to build reactors @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ creating a renewable clean energy solution for world energy demand.The agency believes in a global united approach where the world 's leading scientists collaborate to find the answers to fusion . <h> The ITER Fusion Project <p> ITER means " the way " in Latin . The construction of the ITER test fusion reactor at Cadarache in the South of France is the first significant step towards nuclear fusion the world has seen . ITER Project rank it among the most ambitious science endeavors of our time . The building is underway now on the ITER scientific installation in Saint-Paul-lez-Durance , France . <p> 35 nations are collaborating to build the world 's largest tokamak , a magnetic fusion device that has been designed to prove the feasibility of fusion as a large-scale replicating the nuclear fusion process that happens on the sun . <p> According to ITER.org , " ITER will be the first fusion device to produce net energy . ITER will be the first fusion device to maintain fusion for long periods of time . And ITER will be the first fusion device to test the integrated technologies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ production of fusion-based electricity . Thousands of engineers and scientists have contributed to the design of ITER since the idea for an international joint experiment with fusion was first launched in 1985 . The ITER Members -- China , the European Union , India , Japan , Korea , Russia and the United States -- are now engaged in a 35-year collaboration to build and operate the ITER experimental device , and together bring fusion to the point where a demonstration fusion reactor can be designed . " <p> The ITER construction site at night <p> 100,000 KILOMETRES <p> 100,000 kilometres of niobium-tin ( Nb3Sn ) superconducting strands are necessary for ITER 's toroidal field magnets . Fabricated by suppliers in six ITER Domestic Agencies -- China , Europe , Japan , Korea , Russia and the USA -- production began in 2009 and is currently drawing to a close . Over 400 tonnes of this multifilament wire has been produced for ITER at a rate of about 150 tonnes/year , a spectacular increase in worldwide production capacity ( estimated , before the scale-up for ITER , at a maximum of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Nb3Sn strand produced for ITER would wrap around the Earth at the equator twice . <p> 104 KILOMETRES <p> The heaviest components of the ITER machine will be shipped to the nearest Mediterranean port and then transported along 104 kilometres of specially modified road known as the ITER Itinerary . The dimensions of these components are impressive : the heaviest will weigh nearly 900 tons including the transport vehicle ; the largest will be approximately four storeys -- or 10.6 metres -- high . Some will measure 9 metres across ; others 33 metres long . <p> 150 MILLION ? C <p> The temperature at our Sun 's surface is 6,000 ? C , and at its core -- 15 million ? C. Temperature combines with density in our Sun 's core to create the conditions necessary for the fusion reaction to occur . The gravitational forces of our stars can not be recreated here on Earth , and much higher temperatures are necessary for the laboratory to compensate . In the ITER Tokamak , temperatures will reach 150 million ? C -- or ten times the temperature at the core @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 23,000 tons . The metal contained in the Eiffel Tower ( 7,300 tons ) ca n't compare ... the ITER Tokamak will be as heavy as three Eiffel Towers . The vacuum vessel , with its ports , blanket and divertor , weighs 8,000 tons . Approximately one million components will be integrated into this complex machine . <p> 2X THE THRUST OF A SPACE SHUTTLE LIFT-OFF <p> The structure of the ITER central solenoid -- the large , 1,000-tonne electromagnet in the centre of the machine -- must be strong enough to contain a force equivalent to twice the thrust of the Space Shuttle at take-off . That 's 60 meganewtons or over 6,000 tonnes of force . <p> 360 TONNES <p> Every one of the ITER Tokamak 's 18 D-shaped toroidal field coils will weigh 360 tonnes . The coils will be unloaded from ocean-going vessels before being transported along the ITER Itinerary on radio-controlled transporters. 360 tonnes is the approximate weight of a fully loaded Boeing 747-300 airplane . Each toroidal field coil is 14 metres high and 9 metres wide . <p> 400,000 TONNES <p> The Tokamak @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the future Tokamak Complex . Some 400,000 metric tons will rest on the lower basemat , including the Complex foundations and walls and the ITER Tokamak. 400,000 metric tons is more than the weight of New York 's Empire State Building . <p> 42 HECTARES <p> The main feature of the 180-hectare ITER site in Saint Paul-lez-Durance , southern France , is a man-made level platform that was completed in 2009 . This 42-hectare platform measures 1 kilometre long by 400 metres wide and compares in size to 60 soccer fields . Building began in August 2010 . <p> 5,000 PEOPLE <p> At the peak of ITER construction in 2018-2019 , 5,000 people are expected at ITER ( on the worksite and in the offices ) , up from 1,400 in 2014 . @@7427759 <h> Opinion Column <h> LaFleche : On bugs and climate change <p> If you want to know why we need to take climate change seriously , all you need to do is listen closely to Fiona Hunter . <p> Hunter , a medical entomologist at Brock University , is leading a team of researchers working to determine if local mosquitoes can carry or transmit the Zika virus , a pathogen the World Health Organization has classified as a global threat . <p> During a Friday press conference in which Hunter talked about her research , she noted that Niagara is a " gateway to Canada " for bugs . Because of Niagara 's geography and climate , it can be an ideal place for a new arrival to set up shop . <p> While the rest of the press conference returned to the details of her Zika study , the bit about Niagara being a gateway should n't be forgotten . <p> It 's very likely a sign of things to come . <p> When past outbreaks of insect-borne illness happened in warmer climates , like the current @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to fret over . <p> Canadian weather acted as a barrier to all manner of bugs . Even if they arrived , these insects would not be able to get an ecological foothold . It was just too cold for them to survive . <p> Not anymore . Hunter said the warming of the climate allows many critters , including biting insects that carry pathogens harmful to humans , to migrate north . <p> In the specific case of Zika , this might have already happened . Hunter said a known vector for Zika , the mosquito species Aedes albopictus , has already been found in Ontario . It does n't belong here , so she 's trying to find out if the species has established itself . <p> In short , Zika is the problem de jour but the reality of climate change is the driver behind it for Canada . <p> We still have problems coming to grips with climate change . A recently published University of Montreal study found while 79 per cent of Canadians accept that climate change is real , nearly 40 per cent do n't @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The science on the causes of climate change has been clear for a long time , so it 's disheartening to see so many Canadians denying it . <p> Then again , maybe I should n't be shocked . It 's not the first time Canadian scientific ignorance has been on display . <p> In 2007 , an Angus Reid survey found that 42 per cent of Canadians believe humans and dinosaurs co-existed . That belief is a 65-million-year-old error that essentially means a breathtaking number of Canadians look upon the Flintstones as less of a cartoon and more of a docudrama . <p> Not accepting scientific reality taints the way we approach handling the problem . If we deny that human technology is a significant player in climate change , we 're going to be less likely to consider changing how we use that technology . <p> So we dither about , saying things like " Well , yes , climate change might be a problem but we need to create jobs first , then we can worry about it . " Which is like falling from a plane @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ drink you 'll consider pulling the ripcord . <p> As a result , we make very little progress coping with climate change while disease-carrying insects , which are totally indifferent to human conceits , migrate into our environment . <p> The problem is compounded when you consider how few experts there are tracking how climate change impacts things like insect migration . <p> Brock University is lucky to have someone with Hunter 's chops working there . But she says the community of medical entomologists is very small and shrinking . <p> She told me there just are n't that many people stepping into the field . She 's concerned when her generation retires , there wo n't be enough new entomologists to replace them . <p> Given climate change 's possible negative impacts on human health , we need sentries in the watchtowers . But we do n't teach science nearly well enough , so culturally we do n't respect it much nor place enough emphasis on it . As a consequence , we do n't value people like Hunter as much as we should . <p> If that does @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ so deep there will be no climbing out of it . 
@@7427998 <h> Severe droughts led to fall of the Maya -- Can we learn from this ? <p> One of history 's most enduring mysteries has been the question of what caused the collapse of the Mayan empire . Between 600 and 800 AD , their society rose to its cultural zenith , only to disappear within the next 100 years . <p> The ancient Mayan civilization had its origins in the Yucatan Peninsula , in a vast region of Mesoamerica encompassing what today is southeastern Mexico , all of Guatemala and Belize , and the western portions of Honduras and El Salvador . <p> Carbon dating puts the earliest known occupation of Mayan society in Cuello ( modern-day Belize ) around 2600 BC . The Maya were an impressive civilization , as seen by the art and architecture they left behind . But they were also skilled intellectually , developing the only fully developed writing system in the pre-Columbian Americas . <p> Map showing the extent of the Maya civilization ( red ) , compared to all other Mesoamerica cultures ( black ) . Within Central America and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Their skill with mathematics and astronomy was extraordinary writes the BBC . They used these skills to align their pyramids and temples based on the orientation of the planets and the solar equinoxes . Today we look upon their enduring temples and pyramids in awe , and we wonder what happened to cause this mighty civilization to fall . <p> The decline and collapse of the Maya civilization <p> Archaeologists refer to the decline and collapse of the Maya civilization as the classic Maya collapse . Around 820 AD , the Mayan people began to abandon many of their southern cities , and many cultural activities came to a halt . Within 200 years , the Maya had been assimilated into other Mesoamerican civilizations . <p> This is the structure called " The Observatory " in the Chichen Itza ruins in Mexico . <p> John Romkey from USA <p> How do we know this ? The Maya dated many of their monuments . Prior to 500 AD , few monuments were being built . But between 672 and 750 AD , the number of monuments had grown from about 20 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ After this , the number of monuments being constructed began to fall , dropping to ten by 800 and to zero by 900 AD . <p> What happened during that time when the building of monuments dropped and eventually came to a halt ? There are about 88 different theories as to why the civilization collapsed , ranging from foreign invasion from the south by non-Mayan groups , deforestation and a lack of action by Mayan kings . The most compelling theory to explain the Maya collapse is a prolonged drought , a megadrought . <p> Two years of drought and three failed farming seasons took a vicious toll on landscape and livestock in northwest Mexico . This is a scene from a video filmed in 2012 . <p> World Animal Potection <p> Climate change led to a prolonged severe drought <p> Climate records ( which mostly come from the analysis of cave formations ) show that during the " Classical Age " between about 250 AD and 800 AD , the civilization boomed and rainfall amounts were relatively high . But starting about 820 AD , the region experienced 95 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ decade at a time or more . <p> NCDC/NOAA <p> Thin tropical soils in the Yucatan Peninsula and Pet ? n Basin areas are particularly vulnerable to mega-droughts and were well documented by colonial Spanish officials who recorded the cycles of drought , famine , disease , and war in later years . Since that time , numerous studies have determined climate change and a prolonged and severe drought led to the collapse of the Maya empire . <p> The extensive drought theory provides a comprehensive explanation and all the non-environmental factors , such as war , peasant revolt , changing trade routes and even foreign invasion are all explained as being the result of the drought . The drought theory is becoming accepted as new tools and technology give archaeologists , climate scientists and historians new data to examine . <p> Learning from the past can give us insight into the present <p> " The dry spells of a thousand years ago spanned not years , but generations , " anthropologist Brian Fagan writes in his 2008 book , The Great Warming : Climate Change and the Rise and Fall @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a time of warm climate in the North Atlantic region that may also have been related to other climate events around the world during that time . <p> Fagan looks at other destructive droughts and how they laid waste to some of the world 's great empires , from the Maya in Central America to the Khmer in Cambodia . Research published in PNAS in 2011 , suggests that Khmer 's ancient city of Angkor " might have collapsed " because of an extended drought . <p> Bayon temple , constructed by Angkorian King Jayavarman VII in the late 12th century . The faces may be representations of Buddha , the bodhisattva Lokesvara , Jayavarman VII , or a combination . <p> Mary Beth Day , University of Cambridge <p> Live Science provided some insight into the research , explaining , " recent evidence suggests that prolonged droughts might have been linked to the decline of Angkor -- for instance , tree rings from Vietnam suggest the region experienced long spans of drought interspersed with unusually heavy rainfall . " <p> Collapsed civilizations are interspersed all through our history , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to pestilence , there is a general theme that runs through all of them . It 's a story that goes something like this : When the environment is good , civilizations flourish , agricultural lands are expanded , there is a building boom and populations expand . <p> But what happens when we stretch our ecosystems to their limit ? Add a few stressors , like political , economic or environmental problems , and then add a prolonged drought , and many past societies broke under the strain . But then we have to wonder why some societies collapsed , yet some societies continued to thrive . <p> A sign of the times in California in 2015 . <p> Youtube <p> Kevin Anchukaitis , a Columbia University paleoclimatologist and co-author with Buckley on the 2010 PNAS Angkor paper , has a plausible explanation . " So , were the crops of the Maya or the Angkor Khmer withering in the field during megadroughts ? Perhaps , probably . But I tend to subscribe to the theory -- elegantly described by University of Illinois anthropologist Lisa Lucero -- that it was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , but rather the failure of " elites " and the infrastructure that was part of their power , effectiveness , and control . " <p> So the question for us today is this : Are we as a civilization resilient enough and smart enough to learn from the historical record ? Anchukaitis cautions : " What I think we do n't know yet is what combination of drought characteristics and societal response to them would strain that resilience . To what extent are we immune from the type of drought-civilization synergies that brought about the demise of past complex civilizations ? "

@@7428224 <h> Sea levels are rising faster than they have in 2,800 years <h> Since 1993 the rate has soared to 30 centimetres ( a foot ) per century , up from 3 to 4 centimetres <p> A motorist drives through the brackish sea water that seeps low lying areas in Charleston , S.C . Nuisance flooding , which is from ordinary high tides exacerbated by sea level rise and accompanying land subsidence , is increasing in part due to sea level rise from man-made climate change . ( Stephen B. Morton/Associated Press ) <h> External Links <p> ( Note : CBC does not endorse and is not responsible for the content of external links . ) <p> Sea levels on Earth are rising several times faster than they have in the past 2,800 years and are accelerating because of man-made global warming , according to new studies . <p> Robin Edwards and Benjamin Horton coring a salt marsh in Newfoundland , Canada , to retrieve a record of sea level for the past 2,000 years . ( Ben Horton ) <p> An international team of scientists dug into @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ marsh and coastal wetland in Newfoundland , to chart gently rising and falling seas over centuries and millennia . Until the 1880s and the world 's industrialization , the fastest seas rose was about three to four centimetres ( 1 to 1.5 inches ) a century , plus or minus a bit . During that time global sea level really did n't get much higher or lower than eight centimetres ( three inches ) above or below the 2,000-year average . <p> But in the 20th century the world 's seas rose 14 centimetres ( 5.5 inches ) . Since 1993 the rate has soared to 30 centimetres ( a foot ) per century . And two different studies published Monday in the journal Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences , said by 2100 that the world 's oceans will rise between 28 to 131 centimetres ( 11 to 52 inches ) , depending on how much heat-trapping gas Earth 's industries and vehicles expel . <p> " There 's no question that the 20th century is the fastest , " said Rutgers earth and planetary sciences professor Bob Kopp @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sea levels over the past 3,000 years . " It 's because of the temperature increase in the 20th century which has been driven by fossil fuel use . " <p> To figure out past sea levels and rates of rise and fall , scientists engaged in a " geological detective story , " said study co-author Ben Horton , a Rutgers marine scientist . They went around the world looking at salt marshes and other coastal locations and used different clues to figure out what the sea level was at different times . They used single cell organisms that are sensitive to salinity , mangroves , coral , sediments and other clues in cores , Horton said . On top of that they checked their figures by easy markers such as the rise of lead with the start of the industrial age and isotopes only seen in the atomic age . <p> When Kopp and colleagues charted the sea level rise over the centuries -- they went back 3,000 years , but are n't confident in the most distant 200 years -- they saw Earth 's sea level was on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ man-made <p> Sea level rise in the 20th century is mostly man-made , the study authors said . A separate , not-yet-published study by Kopp and others found since 1950 , about two-thirds of the U.S. nuisance coastal floods in 27 locales have the fingerprints of man-made warming . <p> And if seas continue to rise , as projected , another 45 centimetres ( 18 inches ) of sea level rise is going to cause lots of problems and expense , especially with surge during storms , said study co-author Stefan Rahmstorf of the Potsdam Institute for Climate Impact Research in Germany . <p> " There is such a tight relationship between sea level and temperature , " Horton said . " I wish there was n't , then we would n't be as worried . " <p> An international team of scientists dug into two dozen locations across the globe , including this coastal wetland in Newfoundland , to chart gently rising and falling seas over centuries and millennia . ( Ben Horton ) <p> The link to temperature is basic science , the study 's authors said . Warm @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to specific past eras when temperatures and sea rose and fell together . <p> If greenhouse gas pollution continues at the current pace , both studies project increases of about 57 to 131 centimetres ( 22 to 52 inches ) . If countries fulfil the treaty agreed upon last year in Paris and limit further warming to another 1 C ( 2 degrees Fahrenheit ) , sea level rise would be in the 28 to 56 centimetres ( 11 to 22 inch range ) . <p> Jonathan Overpeck at the University of Arizona , who was n't part of the studies , praised them , saying they show a clear cause and effect between warming and sea level rise . <p> Note : The CBC does not necessarily endorse any of the views posted . @@7439363 <h> Time to chill : University of Alberta 's ice-core storage and research facility receives $2.3-million grant <p> Construction is set to begin next month on a University of Alberta facility to store and do research on 1,700 metres of core ice from Canada 's Arctic . <p> With a $2.3 million grant from the Canada Foundation for Innovation , plus university promises to pay the rest if the Alberta government does n't come through with its share , the facility 's laboratory and two walk-in freezers should be open to researchers in six or seven months , glaciologist Martin Sharp said . <p> Located in the basement of the South Academic Building , the facility is expected to cost about $4 million and will store the ice-core collection currently located in Ottawa at a Natural Resources Canada site . <p> Many of the researchers there are nearing retirement , so moving the collection to the U of A makes sense , Sharp said , since about six out of eight Canadian ice-core researchers with hands-on experience work out of Edmonton . <p> The U of A also @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ organic contaminants found in ice from the atmosphere , and study how the genomics and biology of the ice is affected by climate change , Sharp said . <p> " It 's a unique , one-off opportunity to save and maintain this collection , " said Sharp , a professor in the department of earth and atmospheric sciences and initiating director of the new facility . <p> " At a time when the ice caps that these ( ice cores ) are collected from are disappearing very fast , we should maximize the amount of information we can gain from them about the history of our country . " <p> The ice cores also help scientists determine when and where contaminants trapped in the ice will be released into the environment as the ice melts , helping them plan for the future . <p> Sharp said $1.7 million of the $2.3 million grant is for construction , with the rest used for operations . BEC CITY , Feb. 24 , 2016 /CNW Telbec/ - The Minister of Sustainable Development , Environment and the Fight against Climate Change , David Heurtel , today made public the results of the November 17 , 2015 auction of greenhouse gas ( GHG ) emission allowances that was held jointly with California . This was the sixth joint auction held since the Qu ? bec and California markets were linked on January 1 , 2014 . <p> " The carbon market is the centerpiece of Qu ? bec 's strategy for fighting climate change . In addition to ensuring GHG emission reductions , it is a green fiscal tool that bolsters the re-launching and sustainable development of our economy . By 2020 , more than 3.3 billion dollars will have been invested to support Qu ? bec 's businesses , municipalities and private citizens in their transition to a low-carbon world . Together , we can fight climate change in order to ensure a better quality of life for our children . Let 's do it for them ! The auction generated gross income in the order of 216 million Canadian dollars for Qu ? bec . Total auction income to date has reached nearly 1.2 billion dollars in Qu ? bec . <p> Qu ? bec is aiming to reduce its GHG emissions by 20% below 1990 levels for 2020 , by 37.5% for 2030 , and also adhered to the UNDER 2 MOU framework , aiming at a reduction between 80% and 95% by 2050 . This pathway follows the recommendations for industrialized counties made by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) .

@@7439432 <p> Bill Tuytel walks past hundreds of cedar trees in pots at the front of his Yarrow property . <p> He strolls over a creek lined with vigorous willow trees , and points to where , in January , he found the last spawning coho of the season . <p> Beyond the cedars and the waterway and the driveway to his sprawling Wilson Road home , Tuytel strides across a green field and comes to the spot , slightly elevated over the rest of the land on his 15-or-so acres . <p> This is it . <p> This is where Kinder Morgan 's Trans Mountain oil pipeline crosses his farm . <p> This walk with a reporter on Feb. 18 came one day after Trans Mountain filed its written reply argument to the National Energy Board ( NEB ) , marking the close of the hearing into the $6.8-billion expansion project , which will triple the capacity of the pipeline running 1,153 kilometres from the Alberta oil sands to Burnaby . It was first built in 1953 and purchased by Kinder Morgan Canada in 2005 . <p> A @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ so by the expansion , is Tuytel among those concerned about diluted bitumen flowing across his land , worried about spills in his creek or the aquifer , or the broader issues of climate change and oil use ? <p> Not a bit . <p> " Kinder Morgan is definitely doing all their due diligence in addressing everybody 's concerns , " he said . <p> " I think it 's approved by most of the landowners . " <p> Tuytel is , he thinks , among a silent majority of B.C. residents directly affected by the pipeline who are both in favour of the expansion project for broader economic reasons , but also who are fans of how the company treats them . <p> He talks of Larry , a company representative who comes by with " goodie bags " around Christmas . The company checks for encroachments on the right-of-way with frequent helicopter flights . And he said they also do ground inspections and , internally , run an electrical current through the 63-year-old pipe to stop it from eroding . <p> Then there is the response he 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> " They basically do everything you ask them to , " he said . <p> Tuytel points to a situation last year that really showed him how responsive the company is . Back when it was built , the company essentially dug a trench , laid down pipe , and covered it up with what was dug out , mixing the layers from precious topsoil to gravel . <p> So Tuytel used a de-rocker last year to separate out the gravel on the 20-metre wide strip , then Trans Mountain came in , removed the gravel and put dozens of dump truck loads of pristine topsoil to match the rest of the farm . <p> The cost ? He does n't know , but he estimates given they were running double dump trucks for two weeks , using three flaggers to ensure safe driving on the farm , if he were to do it himself it would have cost close to $20,000 . <p> " It was very well done , " Tuytel said . <p> On top of that , Kinder Morgan owns the right-of-way where the pipeline @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for that in 1953 , Tuytel said the company is repurchasing at today 's values . The company will also rent out farmland that is cutoff by the pipeline right-of-way for a number of years . <p> Tutyel owns Cedarvale Nurseries , which is located at his Wilson Road farm , and Downrite Drilling , also in Chilliwack . ( By way of caveat , it should be noted that his company has indirectly worked for Kinder Morgan on a project near the Westridge Marine Terminal in Burnaby . ) <p> He is in favour of the Trans Mountain Expansion Project as a net benefit for the province . <p> What does he think of those opposed to the project and increased pipelines in general ? <p> " I kind of call them tree huggers , " Tuytel says , with a laugh . <p> In Yarrow , he says he does n't know any neighbours who have said anything against the pipeline project , except the folks at the Yarrow Ecovillage , who he calls " protest type of people . " <p> The Yarrow Ecovillage was an intervenor in the NEB hearing process . <p> " The pipeline really threatens what we are doing here , in the big picture , in the whole picture , " one farmer stated in the ecovillage 's written argument . " It 's going to have a long-term cost in terms of what we do . " <p> And while Tuytel might be right that many or even most landowners are neutral or even positive about the expansion project , there has been a broad swath of residents , landowners , environmental groups , First Nations and municipal governments who have responded across the spectrum , from concern to outright opposition . <p> Even the pro-job , pro-industry BC Liberal government said it can not support the expansion because Kinder Morgan has not provided details on how it will deal with oil spills . <p> Fraser Valley Regional District ( FVRD ) lawyer Maegen Giltrow said at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not be in the public interest to allow the expansion project to be developed and operated at the expense of the local communities that would host the pipeline . @@7439532 <p> The report highlights some alarming statistics pertaining to farm animals . Between 2000 and 2014 , around 100 livestock breeds became extinct . Bringing this forward to today , changes to the planet and modern living mean that 17 percent ( 1,458 ) of the world 's farm animal breeds face the risk of extinction . To add to this , 58 percent could face a similar risk in the future , although precise data is lacking . <p> The root cause for this is appears to be indiscriminate cross-breeding , which causes genetic erosion . This is a common practice across several countries . Other activities impinging upon animal genetic diversity include the wider use of non-native breeds ; weak institutions for regulating farming ; a reduction in traditional livestock production systems ; and the neglect of certain breeds . <p> Worst affected areas are in Europe and North America . This is due to the tendency to focus on highly specialized livestock breeds , meaning the range of different types of animals is very small . <p> In a briefing , FAO Director General Jos @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is a prerequisite for adaptation in the face of future challenges " , and adding that world governments need to " ensure that animal genetic resources are used and developed to promote global food security , and remain available for future generations . " <p> To protect genetic diversity , the report notes that , back in 2007 , there was a United Nation recommendation that each nation establish a gene bank , to conserve genetic information about animals and seeds . As of the end of 2015 , only 10 nations have established such a system . <p> Gene banks a biorepository , designed to preserve genetic material . With plants , this is by freezing cuttings or stocking the seeds . With animals , the process is by freezing of sperm or eggs in cryogenic freezers . <p> To add to animal breeding practices and selection of animal types , the report also considers the impact of climate change ; new pathogens ; population growth ; and migration , with each of these seen as affecting food security .

( LIAM RICHARDS/THE CANADIAN PRESS ) <p> The federal government can expect to pay nearly a billion dollars a year in disaster relief for extreme weather events -- far more than the $100-million it has been budgeting -- as the increasing frequency and intensity of hurricanes , winter storms and especially floods take a greater toll , the Parliamentary Budget Officer says in a report . <p> But , as climate change inevitably alters traditional weather patterns , politicians and environmental experts say the most important @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ advance where the worst storm damage is likely to occur and to take steps to offset it . <p> The destruction caused by extreme weather is a " problem that can be , with the right kind of planning , not eliminated but reduced by mitigation policies and programs , " Public Safety Minister Ralph Goodale , whose department is in charge of federal disaster assistance , told The Globe and Mail on Thursday . <p> The Disaster Financial Assistance Arrangements ( DFFA ) program was created in 1970 and is used by Public Safety Canada to reimburse provinces and individuals for the damage that results from disasters , such as the Toronto area ice storm and the massive flood in southern Alberta and eastern British Columbia that occurred in 2013 . <p> The cost of such an event is shared with the provinces , with the federal contribution increasing until the price tag reaches $15 multiplied by the province 's population , after which Ottawa pays 90 per cent . <p> While the Public Safety Department has been budgeting $100-million annually for this disaster relief , the report shows that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2013-14 and $305-million in 2014-15 . And the DFAA estimates that its transfers to the provinces will be higher going forward : $848-million in 2015-16 , $590-million in 2016-17 and $580-million in 2017-18 . <p> But the Parliamentary Budget Officer report says that even those numbers are too low . <p> Using data from the DFFA , the Insurance Bureau of Canada , Swiss Re and Risk Management Solutions Inc. , the PBO estimates that the DFAA will have to pay an average of $229-million a year for hurricanes , convective storms and winter storms and an additional $673-million annually for floods . Those two numbers add up to an estimated bill of $902-million annually . <p> The PBO report says the largest damage from floods is expected to occur in the prairie provinces because so much infrastructure in Manitoba , Saskatchewan and Alberta has been built on floodplains and , unlike in Ontario and Quebec , the management of those floodplains has been left to municipalities , which do not have good data or planning to deal with major natural disasters . <p> The Insurance Bureau of Canada issued a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ affirmed the bureau 's research , " which shows that climate change is a real and present danger costing government , and Canadians , hundreds of millions of dollars every year . " <p> Mr. Goodale said it 's clear that significant weather events are big problems that are only going to get bigger , which is why the Liberal government has promised to create a green infrastructure fund of $20-billion over the next 10 years to make communities more resilient . The initial investments from that fund could be announced in the budget that is expected late next month . <p> Blair Feltmate , head of the Intact Centre on Climate Adaptation at the University of Waterloo , said that from 1983 to 2008 , Canadians insurers paid $200-million to $500-million annually for losses resulting from natural catastrophes . But , since 2009 , there has never been a year when those claims dipped below $1-billion , and in 2013 the payout was $3.4-billion , he said . <p> Recognizing that climate change can be slowed by the reduction of greenhouse-gas emissions but not entirely stopped , Dr. Feltmate said @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that is in the system now and that will be realized even more so going forward . " <p> That must include the production of up-to-date floodplain maps that will indicate where the water from major weather events is going to go , he said . And those maps must look forward " to make sure that we are out of harm 's way from the big storms that will occur 25 to 50 years from now , " he said .

@@7445086 <p> The quickest way to annoy an engaged citizen is to have them troop down to their local town hall to ask for a change in speed limit or a composting program , only to be told it 's out of their councillors ' control because it 's the jurisdiction of a higher level of government . <p> " We are in a different era now , where people are n't willing to put up with those stock answers , " said Suzanne Roy . " They say ' We elected you to change things . We expect you to change things . ' " <p> Roy should know . The mayor of Ste-Julie , a city of 30,000 located 10 kilometres east of Montreal , she hears the queries on a regular basis , often while doing her groceries . She 's also in her second one-year mandate as president of the Union of Quebec Municipalities ( UMQ ) , which counts roughly 300 of the province 's 1,111 municipalities as members , representing 80 per cent of the province 's population . <p> The power of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the last two years in large part because the mayors of Montreal and Quebec City made a concerted effort to organize the long-divided cities and towns into a cohesive unit with the power to influence Quebec , and Canada . Strength in numbers is bearing fruit , Roy said , evidenced by new provincial legislation in the works promised this autumn that will grant municipalities greater authority to decide their own affairs and make a difference in the lives of their citizens . <p> In a meeting with the Montreal Gazette 's editorial board Thursday , Roy outlined some of the main changes municipalities are seeking . <p> Municipal salaries <p> Salaries account for 50 per cent or more of many municipalities ' budgets , but city officials said negotiation laws in the province favoured municipal unions , with the result city employees make on average 39 per cent more than their provincial public service counterparts , Roy said . Under Quebec 's new fiscal pact with its municipalities , still under negotiation , municipalities are giving up millions in transfer payments from the province in exchange for the right for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ break down . Unions say they are being robbed of their bargaining rights , and vow to fight any such law in court . <p> Red tape <p> For a $12,000 provincial infrastructure grant , municipalities have been ordered to pay for a $5,000 external review , " which does n't leave much left over for infrastructure , " Roy said . Permission to build with provincial funds can involve filling out four different forms for four different government ministries . The mayor of Drummondville recently found himself filling out a seven-page form to qualify for an $800 grant . The extra work is estimated to waste 2.5 million municipal man hours a year . The Quebec government is working to reduce the bureaucracy , Roy says <p> Climate change <p> Infrastructure like bridges and sewage conduits are often built to survive " once every hundred years " floods or rains storms . Ste-Julie has experienced two such rain storms in the last decade , Roy said , and more are expected shortly thanks to the effects of global warming . With their dense populations , municipalities hold the keys to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has emissions . <p> Ste-Julie started offering free local public transit to all residents two years ago . The biggest surprise was how many teenagers use it , freed from the constraints of the mommy and daddy taxi system . Those are young people who will be trained for life to embrace public transit , Roy notes . In terms of recycling , composting , using electrical vehicles in their public transit and municipal fleets or granting benefits to developers who build green , municipalities are where the difference is made worldwide . The Quebec union of municipalities signed an agreement with its Ontario counterpart to share best practices . And to apply pressure to their provincial and federal governments to get the aid they need to create change . <h> Comments <p> We encourage all readers to share their views on our articles and blog posts . @@7445234 <h> Related Stories <p> Buried in the fine print of the new legislation , released Thursday , Ontario has limited greenhouse gas emission allowances at 142.2 million metric tonnes in 2017 , gradually reducing it by 4.2 per cent each year until it hits 124.6 million metric tonnes by 2020 . <p> The province is bringing in the cap-and-trade program on Jan. 1 , 2017 , which will create limited tradable allowances in the carbon market . <p> An estimated 100 companies and organizations will receive exemptions , or free allowances , over the first four years -- which the government dubs " transitional assistance , " in sectors such as cement , petrochemicals , steel and mining . <p> Non-profit organizations including the University of Toronto and York University and some hospitals , which generate their own power , will always receive free allowances , though if they lower emissions then they can sell unneeded allowances . <p> According to the most recent data , in 2013 , Ontario emitted an estimated 171 million metric tonnes -- but that was before the province closed the last coal-fired @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ joining with other jurisdictions like Quebec and California in a cap-and-trade system to reduce industrial carbon emissions . <p> " Under the program , Ontario would cap the amount of greenhouse gas pollution that businesses and institutions can emit , " said Finance Minister Charles Sousa says in his budget speech . " Companies could respond by investing in clean technologies to become more efficient , burning fewer fossil fuels or buying additional carbon credits . " <p> The Liberals say putting a price on carbon will bring in $600 million more than originally anticipated -- as much as $1.9 billion in 2017 , with $478 million occurring in 2016-17 . The funds will be earmarked to efforts to fight climate change . <p> Based on the Western Climate Initiative with California and Quebec , the carbon price in Ontario would be roughly $18 a tonne , compared to $30 a tonne in British Columbia . Alberta has said it will bring a $20 a tonne carbon levy that will grow to $30 in 2018 . <p> Conservative Leader Patrick Brown , who said he takes climate change seriously , questioned @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Cap-and-trade is a new revenue grab for the government , " Brown said . " It is going to cause everyone to pay more . . . I think the majority in this province will be very disappointed if it is n't dedicated funds . " <p> Brown worries that the funds can go to pet projects , regardless of whether it reduces greenhouse gas emissions . <p> NDP Leader Andrea Horwath said her party supports the idea of a cap-and-trade program , but worries about the transparency and fairness for lower-income Ontarians or those in northern Ontario . <p> As well , she argued that big companies that are bigger polluters are getting an initial break , while ordinary citizens are not getting any help . <p> For consumers , the government says the cap-and-trade program will translate into about $5 a month in higher home heating costs , or 3.3 cents per cubic metre , and $8 a month in higher gasoline costs , or 4.3 cents a litre . <p> The government acknowledges there will be a " small negative change " to GDP growth due to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> It said GDP will grow by 11 per cent between 2015 and 2020 without cap and trade -- but with the climate change initiatives , the economy will reach the same level 1.5 days later each year , or six days cumulatively by 2020.

Moreover , against public outcry , the Wynne government has held the line on austerity cuts and hydro privatization in order to fund its infrastructure plans . <p> " The 2016 Ontario Budget throws public services under the infrastructure bus when it should be presenting Ontarians with a roadmap for growing the economy and reversing the backslide to inequality , " said Buckley . " Piecemeal improvements to student financial assistance , disability support and other positive measures wo n't help reduce inequality in Ontario if families are squeezed by precarious work , shrinking public services and increasing user fees . " <p> In its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to abandon its balanced budget fixation in favour of new investments in job creation , restoring public services and making sure that banks and corporations pay their fair share . The OFL cited a report released by the Ontario Common Front in November 2015 , demonstrating that , by nearly every measure , Ontario is trailing every other province in income equality and poverty reduction . Among the most alarming findings are : <p> Ontario has experienced a 50% increase in the duration of unemployment , making its long-term unemployment the second worst in Canada ; <p> 1.7 million people are now earning within $4 of the minimum wage ; <p> There has been a 38% increase in poverty in Ontario over the past 20 years and nearly one in five Ontario children live in poverty ; <p> Young Ontario families pay up to $19,000 a year for child care , the highest costs in Canada ; and <p> University tuition fees have outpaced inflation by 601% while per student funding is dead last ; and <p> Ontario funds all of its social programs at the lowest rate in Canada . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ today 's budget and mark modest improvements that will be a benefit to working people . Among the budget announcements being welcomed by the OFL are : <p> A commitment to tackling climate change through the introduction of a " cap and trade " program ; <p> Concrete measures to make higher education more affordable , in particular through the introduction of grants that will render tuition fees free for students whose household income is below $50,000 ; <p> A lifting of the freeze on hospital funding through a new injection of $345 million ; and <p> A " basic income pilot project , " the details of which will be determined in 2016 . <p> " Wynne has given us one step forward , two steps back . Without increasing social program funding above inflation , this budget will further cement Ontario 's last place status for social program funding , income equality and poverty reduction , " said Buckley . " The shortcomings of today 's budget make it all the more essential for this government to move forward with bold plans to close Ontario 's gender wage gap @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ worker is lifted out of poverty and fairness becomes the law of the land . " <p> The Ontario Federation of Labour ( OFL ) represents 54 unions and one million workers in Ontario . For information , visit www.OFL.ca and follow @OFLabour on Facebook and Twitter . 
@@7445387 <h> Inside Montreal duo Atomic3 's magical Island of Warmth <p> At the Montreal en Lumi ? re festival this weekend , not far from the candy-coloured lights of the ferris wheel , the zip line , and the urban slide , a bonfire will be raging at the Place des Festivals . The more people gather to dance around it , the louder the tribal music they 'll hear , and the higher the flames will go -- or seem to go , encased in rods of glass . <p> " People do n't know each other , but for a few moments , they are creating something together . " - Atomic3 's F ? lix Dagenais on how their installations bring people together <p> The Island of Warmth , by Atomic3 , Montreal 's Louis-Xavier Gagnon-Lebrun and F ? lix Dagenais , is a trompe l'oeil . The " flames " are made by incandescent bulbs , and the music is pre-recorded . Sensors hidden under the fire , beneath the platform where people dance , and under the rocking chairs surrounding them , let @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ As they congregate , they create their own warmth . <p> " At first , they are very curious and shy , " says Gagnon-Lebrun . " They come closer , and as soon as they see someone doing something like jumping , they try to jump a little bit . " <p> Dagenais chimes in : " As the fire becomes more musical , they get caught up in the happy spirit . " <p> This playful interaction -- with their work , and by extension with the city around it -- motivates Atomic3 . Gagnon-Lebrun and Dagenais met while both were working as lighting designers for Robert Lepage . Inspired by the way Lepage uses technology and innovative sets to tell stories , they decided , Gagnon-Lebrun explains , to " get out of the black box of the theatre and tell those stories in public spaces . " <p> Their first work together , ? clats de verre ( 2011 ) , encouraged visitors to bounce two illuminated spring-mounted boxes in the Place ? milie-Gamelin to control music , animations projected on a nearby building , and lights @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> The next year , they designed Iceberg , a series of aluminum archways running through the Place des Festivals and Place des Arts , whose blue lights turned red as people walked under them -- suggesting the effects of human-created climate change . Sounds were triggered as well : as icebergs melt , says Gagnon-Lebrun , " they make music . There are crevices in them that work a bit like a pipe organ , with air going through . " The farther south one walked along Iceberg 's arches , the more traditionally " musical " the sounds became . <p> ? Atomic3 built on the musical theme for Maestro ( 2015 ) , an uncharacteristically summery work where visitors to the Place des Festivals could walk up to a podium , pick up a baton and " conduct " fountains . Sensors on a music stand let them raise and lower water-jets and the volume of surrounding orchestral music . The work was inspired by a YouTube video of a three-year-old 's spirited " conducting " of Beethoven ; a new version of Maestro this May will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Upcoming installations work on larger scales , with bigger crowds . Nu ? e de verre , set for later this year , will suspend panes of glass from the ceilings of Trudeau International Airport 's boarding gates and arrivals halls , casting coloured shapes that will shift according to how many people are walking through . And for next year 's Illuminart Festival , celebrating Montreal 's 375th birthday , Atomic3 will work with Moment Factory ( who did stage projections for Madonna 's 2012 Super Bowl halftime performance ) to animate lights on the perennially busy Jacques Cartier bridge across the Saint Lawrence . Their plan is still in progress , but Gagnon-Lebrun says they hope to use mobile phone data " to check the pulse of the city . The lighting gets pretty quiet if it 's cold outside , people are all inside , and there 's no hockey game -- they 're not connecting . If there is a concert at Osheaga in the summer , the bridge will be going crazy . " <p> Atomic3 see all of their work as a means @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the connections it creates , and even of creating some themselves . <p> " There 's a challenge to make anonymous people play together and interact , " says Dagenais , " and that 's one thing we are pretty proud of with The Island of Warmth . People do n't know each other , but for a few moments , they are creating something together and are so happy . " <p> Note : The CBC does not necessarily endorse any of the views posted . @@7445393 <h> Five things to know about the Ontario budget <p> TORONTO -- Ontario 's Liberal government delivered its 2016-2017 budget on Thursday . Here are five things you need to know about the fiscal plan . <p> POST-SECONDARY STUDENTS : College and university students from low-income families stand to benefit the most from the Liberal government 's fiscal plan . Starting in the 2017-18 school year , the province will create a new grant -- called the Ontario Student Grant . Under the new system , tuition will be free for students from Ontario families with incomes up to $50,000 who attend any college or university in Canada . More than half of students from families with incomes up to $83,000 will receive non-repayable grants that exceed the average tuition -- mostly students who live on their own . No Ontario student will receive less through the new grant than they are currently eligible for through the Ontario Tuition Grant . <p> CAP AND TRADE : Proceeds from the cap-and-trade system -- which is designed to reduce Ontario 's greenhouse gas emissions -- are projected to be $1.9 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of $1.3 billion -- and will be used to invest in green projects and climate change initiatives . However , the program comes at a cost to Ontario ratepayers . The average natural gas cost to households will increase $5 per month , while the price of gasoline will go up about 4.3 cents per litre -- or about $8 per month . <p> CIGARETTES AND WINE : The prices of cigarettes and wine are going up . There will be a $3 increase in the price of a carton of 200 cigarettes , effective at 12:01 a.m . Friday , and the tobacco tax will keep rising at the rate of inflation each year over the next five years . The minimum price for a bottle of wine rises to $7.95 over the next three years , and there will be a series of increases in the ad valorem tax on wine , starting with a two percentage point hike in June , another two points in 2017 and 2018 , and a one-point hike in 2019 <p> DEFICIT : The budget deficit for fiscal year 2015-16 is expected to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ estimate of $7.5 billion . The deficit for 2016-17 is projected to come in at $4.6 billion and be eliminated the following fiscal year , as promised . The sale of government assets and proceeds from carbon pricing are expected to help the Liberals meet that goal . <p> NET DEBT : Ontario 's net debt , which currently sits at $296.1 billion , is expected to hit $308 billion in 2016-17 , making the province the largest sub-national borrower in the world . Interest payments will cost $11.8 billion -- or $22,103 for every person in Ontario -- and are expected to increase to $13.1 billion by 2018-19.

Every little bit helps . <p> Those are among dozens of projects , large and small , in the township 's action plan . Solar panels have been installed where possible and as of November all street lights use LED bulbs . <p> One innovation targets township residents who were adding to the cost of recycling by tossing beer and liquor bottles in their blue boxes . @@7450559 <h> Financial cost of major weather events rising for Canadian taxpayers <p> A new report is putting a massive price tag on the cost of climate change in Canada -- with the tally reaching hundreds of millions of dollars each year . <p> The costs related to cleaning up devastating floods , the carnage from hurricanes and the damage left in the wake of intense winter storms has exploded to nearly $900 million annually , up from an average of $850 million in previous years , said Parliamentary Budget Officer Jean-Denis Frechette . <p> And it 's taxpayers who have to foot the bill for the costs , which are only expected to increase as our weather continues to deteriorate due to climate change . <p> " Not only the number but the intensity of the weather events , " will increase , Frechette told CTV News . <p> The report found most of the spending went towards flood relief in the Prairies , where the PBO also found most flood maps are out of date . <p> " If you have economic development or housing projects for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 're going to have huge losses associated with the floods , " Frechette said . <p> Public payouts are high , Frechette said , because private flood insurance is rare . It is currently only available in Ontario and Alberta , and even there just recently . So when disaster strikes in the form of flooding , it is often the government that steps in to bail out residents . <p> And even where flood insurance is available , it 's most expensive where it is needed most . In high risk areas , flood insurance coverage up to $1 million averages about $18,000 per year and typically comes with a $100,000 deductible -- and that does n't include coverage of contents . <p> " Flood operates differently than other home hazards such as fire because for certain places we know , where the flooding is going to happen and it 's going to happen there year after year , " said Craig Stewart of the Insurance Bureau of Canada . <p> The report highlights a point many are making -- that as the federal government prepares to invest billions of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it should focus on erecting buildings that can withstand the extreme weather that is expected to increase in frequency due to climate change . <p> " Disaster relief is a Band-Aid not a cure , and we need to do this sort of proactive spending , " said Simon Donner , associate professor of geography at the University of British Columbia . <p> The Liberal government had committed to doing just that as part of their green infrastructure election pledge . However , a spokesperson for the minister said not to expect details on such projects until next month 's federal budget . 
@@7450787 <p> The election of the new Trudeau government has given rise to many hopes . Among these are the hope that Canada can once again become a peacekeeping nation after its long and sad experience of war in Afghanistan . Those who advocate a return to peacekeeping point to Canada 's long history of involvement in UN and other missions dating back to the late 1940s . And they are quite right to derive some pride from Canada 's distinguished record in these operations . From Palestine to Cyprus , from Kashmir to Cambodia , from Namibia to El Salvador , Canadian service men and women made a highly useful contribution to the preservation of ceasefires and to the prevention of conflict escalation . That said , the story of Canada 's involvement in peacekeeping is not an entirely happy one . <p> Those nostalgic for an earlier era in peacekeeping tend to forget or overlook some of the darker episodes . Thus Canada participated in the UN mission in Congo in the early 1960s , a mission that ended in total failure . Canada 's contingent in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ unceremoniously expelled in 1967 . Several Canadian peacekeepers were killed when Turkey invaded Cyprus in 1974 . More recently , Canada 's involvement in Somalia resulted in a public relations disaster for the Canadian Forces . And Canada 's engagements in peacekeeping in the former Yugoslavia in the mid-1990s produced frustration , humiliation and casualties for Canadian troops . Not an entirely rosy picture . <p> Those who nostalgically harken back to the glory days of Canadian peacekeeping also tend to ignore the fact that the world and its conflicts have changed dramatically in the last 25 years . The last classical iteration of Canada 's criteria for participation in peacekeeping operations appeared in the Canadian government 's Defence White Paper of 1987 . It makes for enlightening reading in 2016 . The criteria listed were : <p> * whether there is a clear and enforceable mandate ; <p> * whether the principal antagonists agree to a ceasefire ; <p> * whether the principal antagonists agree to Canadian participation ; <p> * whether the arrangements are likely to serve the cause of peace and lead to a political settlement ; <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ appropriate to the mandate ; <p> * whether there is a single identifiable authority competent to support the operation and influence the disputants ; <p> The hallmarks of classical peacekeeping as practised in the second half of the 20th century were variously : presence , interposition , observation , reporting , negotiation and persuasion . UN forces were expected to be strictly neutral in their dealings with the opposing forces , and to use their weapons only in self-defence . This sort of peacekeeping assumed that the parties to a given conflict were prepared to observe a ceasefire and gave their consent to the presence , the mandate and the functions of the peacekeeping mission . <p> The situation could not be more different in the year of grace 2016 . Virtually none of the UN 's current peacekeeping missions would meet even half of the Canadian government 's traditional criteria for participation . In most cases , there is no agreed ceasefire in place , the peacekeepers do not operate with the consent of all of the parties to the conflict , and more often than not they find themselves @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ parties . Far from using their weapons only in self-defence , they are frequently obliged to fight bands of rebels or insurgents in order to defend vulnerable civilian populations . In fact , most of these are not peacekeeping operations but peace enforcement operations with significant risks of casualties . What is more , none of them seems to be leading to a durable peace agreement . <p> For purposes of illustration , it is instructive to examine the record and performance of the UN 's largest current deployment , the so-called Stabilization Mission in the Democratic Republic of the Congo . Under one name or another , it has been in existence since 1999 and now numbers more than 20,000 troops . It has scored some minor successes in protecting civilian populations , but overall its record is one of dismal failure . The civil wars in Congo have led to the deaths of some five million people and to the rape of hundreds of thousands of women . Those wars go on and the UN force has been unable to bring them to an end . In fact , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ many hostile and brutal factions involved over such a large territory , that the UN force 's task is a mission impossible . <p> But beyond its inability to accomplish its mission over a 15-year period , there are other aspects of the Congo operation that are worth noting . It is less a peacekeeping than a peace enforcement operation with its troops often engaged in combat with various rebel factions . The UN force has in fact suffered several hundred casualties . It has also been involved in child prostitution scandals . And it is indirectly supportive of a Congolese government notable for corruption , electoral fraud and human rights violations . <p> Given all of this , would Canada seriously consider contributing troops to the UN mission in Congo ? After all , it is a mission that has been shunned by most of Canada 's traditional partners in peacekeeping . The list includes Norway , Sweden , Denmark , Austria and Australia . The major contributors to the Congo force are developing countries such as India , Bangladesh , Pakistan and Egypt , which derive considerable economic benefits @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is not true of the Congo mission alone . It is also true of the other major UN deployments in Sub-Saharan Africa , in Sudan , South Sudan , the Central African Republic and Chad . These are certainly not missions in which Canada could hope to make a serious contribution to international peace and security . <p> Once these large missions are excluded , the options for a renewed Canadian involvement in UN peacekeeping are somewhat limited . Canada could , of course , consider rejoining one of the two venerable old UN missions from which it withdrew after some 30 years of participation : the UN Force in Cyprus or the UN Disengagement Observer Force in the Golan Heights . But these are missions that managed to carry on quite satisfactorily without Canadian troops ; there is no obvious need here . <p> There is in reality only one UN mission to which Canada could make a useful contribution . And that is the UN force in Mali . Constituted after the French army 's successful efforts to halt the advance of Islamist insurgents in Mali , this force @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . And this is generally regarded as an important security issue , since it is feared that Mali could become a base from which Islamist extremists could launch attacks against western Europe . As currently constituted , the UN force in Mali is too small and ill-equipped to carry out its mission successfully . It could make good use of Canadian troops and of Canadian transport and logistics support . But , pace the nostalgics , this is not a classical peacekeeping mission ; it will involve combat against incursions by Islamist extremists . <p> Envoi <p> The new Trudeau government is absolutely right to want to re-engage with the United Nations in a wide variety of endeavours ranging from humanitarian assistance to climate change . Its options for becoming more actively involved in UN peacekeeping are , however , distinctly limited . And whatever it chooses to do , it will not be grandpa 's peacekeeping . <p> Louis A. Delvoie is a Fellow in the Centre for International and Defence Policy at Queen 's University .

" Our city is already doing a lot in bee farming and protection , and we need to serve as a leading example to the rest of Canada . " <p> City council will consider the motion on March @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ end of the month . <h> UN Reports warns over dangers of pollinator loss <p> The buzz around Toronto 's drive to save the bees comes as the UN 's Intergovernmental Science-Policy Platform on Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services ( IPBES ) issues a new report warning about the catastrophic implications of the death of pollinators world-wide . <p> The report , issued February 26 , 2016 , cites the importance of the world 's more than 20,000 species of wild bees in food production - 75% and up to $577 billion USD worth of crops annually depend , at least in part , on animal pollination . <p> It is dire news , then , that the report estimates 16 per cent of vertibrate pollinators are currently threatened with extinction , and more than 40 per cent of invertebrate species threatened locally . <p> In a release by the IPBES , Vice-chair Sir Robert Watson commented on the causes : <p> " Wild pollinators in certain regions , especially bees and butterflies , are being threatened by a variety of factors . Their decline is primarily due to changes in land @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ invasive species , diseases and pests , and climate change . " <p> The report authors - which was compiled by 77 experts combing over more than 3000 scientific papers for two years - suggest the best ways to bolster the bee population include crop diversity , decreasing the use of pesticides , increasing the number of hives worldwide , educational outreach for farmers , scientists and the public , and mitigating the effects of climate change .

@@7451016 <h> Opinion Column <h> Diving into the shallow end of the talent gene pool <h> Topics <p> In the last week of the Duffy trial - yes , it 's still going on - we are reminded of the " boys in short pants . " In the interests of gender parity , let 's expand it to include ' girls . ' And , we live in dangerous times of political correctness , so I mean the men and women that work for cabinet ministers . <p> The expression seems odd . Why are their pants short ? The phrase has only been around since 2012 . Conservative members of Parliament were very unhappy about the youthfulness of Harper 's staff . <p> The political staff gene pool in Canada is very small . It is basically the same people following partisan governments around the country . <p> Work for a Conservative government in Ottawa , end up working for the Progressive Conservative government in Toronto . Work for the NDP in government anywhere , work for the NDP government in Alberta . <p> Work for the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for the Trudeau government in Ottawa . <p> The people that brought you McGuinty and Wynne now bring you Justin Trudeau . Some may take comfort in that , but quite honestly it scares the hell out of me . <p> Premier McGuinty ( aka " Premier Dad " ) started out by breaking every promise he ever made in the first six months of office . He morphed into a scandal plagued premier making billion dollar gas plants moves to win elections and , under his watch , computer hard drives being wiped clean . <p> Premier Wynne is out to save us from ourselves . Have you enjoyed lower gas prices ? Well good , because Wynne is adding four cents to a litre to save the world . <p> The gene pool of political talent is narrowed down even further by two things : really good people never join an office and other people leave to make better money after two years . <p> Anyone that has been around a political campaign knows that the top people have no interest in working in government . There is far @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> When the thrill of setting up a new government is over , there is even more money to be made outside of government as a former " insider . " <p> It 's something that no one talks about . It 's close to influence peddling . <p> The small group of men and women around a minister start becoming younger . They love the rush of " important meetings , " flying to Paris , cell phones and ministerial cars . <p> Polished and impeccably dressed ( I have never seen a pair of short pants on any of them ) you see them in media scrums recording their minister 's comments or walking with their masters down the halls of power . <p> They have one job : protect their boss at any cost - even ( as Nigel Wright found out ) at the risk of being fired . <p> The worst type of political staffer is someone that has never been through a resounding defeat and someone that will not privately take on their boss . <p> Defeat builds character . You go from being @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and can spend millions of dollars , to being a nobody . <p> Being able to argue with your boss - privately - is important . People like Harris , Harper , Mulroney , Trudeau have huge egos and they want things done that are just plain dumb , ill-conceived , ham-handed . <p> You need a staff that is going to go toe-to-toe with them , pound desks , curse and scream . <p> Politics changes people . Ministers and prime ministers easily morph into tyrants and there are a lot of younger people that are afraid to take them on . I 've seen it a million times . <p> The worst situation is when you have a group that is very like-minded . They see an issue as " The Cause . " That issue will be their legacy piece . There are bigger issues , more pressing issues - jobs , the economy , resources - but their issue ( climate change ) will be the most important one . <p> In Ontario , our premier and our prime minister , have decided that , regardless of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bigger polluters , we will lead on climate change . <p> We will pay more for gas and home heating . We will forgo revenue from oil in favour of imports from regimes that trample human rights , all so we can be happy in 2030 . If people will not see the light , they will be shown the light with taxes , regulation and heavy handed measures , consequences be damned . <p> Premier Wynne says that the cost of doing nothing would be greater . I highly doubt that taxpayers find comfort in those words right here , right now . <p> Sadly , when you look at Trudeau 's staff ranks , no one will disagree because many are from the McGuinty/Wynne government . <p> I am reminded that " great minds think alike " but I am more inclined to believe that " fools seldom differ . "
