@@7455886 <p> rabble blogs are the personal pages of some of Canada 's most insightful progressive activists and commentators . All opinions belong to the writer ; however , writers are expected to adhere to our guidelines . We welcome new bloggers -- contact us for details . <h> Christopher Majka <p> Christopher Majka studied at Mount Alison and Dalhousie Universities , the Pushkin Institute in Moscow and was a guest researcher at the Edward Gray Institute at Oxford University . He has written extensively for many national and international publications . His scientific work includes over 150 scientific papers and contributions to nine books . He is a review editor for two international publications , a collaborator of the Kaltenbach Lab and director of Democracy : Vox Populi . Majka was a recipient of the Tom Brydges Award from the Ecological Monitoring and Assessment Network , and was included as one of Canadian Geographic 's Environmental Scientists of the Year in 2010 . <h> Exclusive : Elizabeth May on climate change , electoral , political , and monetary reform , sexual assault and much more <p> Like this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ keep stories like these coming . <p> Elizabeth May is once again alone . For a period of almost two years she was joined on the Green Party parliamentary benches in Ottawa by former New Democrat and former independent MP Bruce Hyer . However , Hyer lost his seat in the 2015 election leaving the MP for Saanich-Gulf Islands as once again the sole Green Party representative in the House of Commons . <p> However , May is used to being a one-person caucus and it does n't seem to have affected her level of political effectiveness , and it certainly has not diminished her political enthusiasm . Indeed despite , or perhaps because , of her position as a partisan outlier on the parliamentary playing field she has been able to blaze a distinctive path . May is a straight-talker , unencumbered by party whips and unafraid to call it like she sees it . In a political universe too often dominated by party lines and talking points , spokespeople and handlers , protocol and general political fustiness , May is a refreshing bolt out of the clear blue sky @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ you wo n't hear any bafflegab from her . <p> Full disclosure : I 've known Elizabeth May for over 30 years , from the time when we first worked together on a campaign to stop the aerial spraying of herbicides in Nova Scotia and collaborated as members of the Ecology Action Center 's Forestry Committee . Over those years I 've seen her political acumen and environmental dedication grow in leaps and bounds , from her years as a grassroots activist , lawyer , senior policy advisor of Progressive Conservative Environment Minister Tom McMillan , executive director of the Sierra Club of Canada , and finally leader of the Green Party of Canada . It 's been a remarkable arc . <p> One of the definitions of gadfly in my dusty dictionary is , " a person who threatens the status quo of a society by posing novel and potentially upsetting questions , usually directed at authorities . " This notion originates with Socrates who considered his role as ? ? ? ? ( myops or gadfly ) in Athenian society , " to sting people and whip them into @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> I sometimes think that in addition to a Speaker of the House of Commons there should also be the position of Parliamentary Gadfly -- a post that May would be admirably suited to occupy . While some political leaders may find her probing " upsetting " and take periodic swipes at her , the threat of being stung by her command of the facts and rhetorical skills is more than a little salutary for Canadian democracy . <p> For a candid view of the state of Canadian democracy , the health of parliamentary institutions , the results of climate negotiations , or almost anything else of consequence crossing the Canadian social , political , or environmental radar , there is no one more engaging or salubrious to speak with than Elizabeth May . I caught up with her while she was in Halifax to speak about democratic reform at the Schulich School of Law at her former alma mater , Dalhousie University . <p> Paris Climate Change Convention <p> Christopher G. Majka : You attended COP-21 the 2015 Paris Climate Conference . In the aftermath of the conference , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ was a success or failure . On the one hand 195 countries entered into the Paris Agreement , the object of which is to keep anthropogenic climate change to " well below " 2 degrees Celsius ( above pre-industrial levels ) by end of the 21st century , and indeed to " pursue efforts " to limit this temperature increase to 1.5 degrees Celsius . <p> On the other hand , experts who have tallied up the current greenhouse gas reduction targets of these participating states , estimate that they would lead to a temperature increase of something on the order of 2.5 -- 3.7 degrees Celsius -- far in excess of what everyone now understands are tolerable limits to temperature increase that does not result in enormously disruptive climate change . <p> While there are no binding targets unless and until the treaty has been ratified under the ratification formula ( 55 parties who produce over 55% of the global Greenhouse gas emissions ) and important CO2 producing activities like international air and shipping are not included in the Paris Agreement targets , on the other hand both the United @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ largest global greenhouse gas emitters , prominently participated and arguably broke a logjam that had previously stymied efforts to reach a meaningful global consensus on climate change . <p> There are many other " on the one hand , on the other hand " comparisons that can be adduced to an evaluation of the Paris Climate Conference . What is your view ? Is the Paris glass half empty or half full ? <p> Elizabeth E. May : Had this agreement failed , if this had been another " Copenhagen " we would have had no chances ever to get another agreement on a multilateral global basis . This was the last chance . <p> In my view we could have got this agreement in 2009 in Denmark ( Copenhagen Climate Change Conference -- December , 2009 ) if we had n't had a dysfunctional host government . Denmark 's right-wing prime minister ( Lars L ? kke Rasmussen ) had his own cadre of experts doing their own thing and not talking to Connie Hedegaard who was the Danish Minister of the Environment Note : Connie Hedegaard was the president @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from December 7-16 , 2009 at which point she left the chair to Rasmussen for the final three days of the conference. , so there were two competing groups . <p> CGM : And that made such a substantial difference in terms of the outcome of the conference ? <p> EEM : Oh , huge . When Rasmussen took the chair of the conference away from Hedegaard he did n't know how to run the UN negotiations ; ignored countries like Venezuela with Hugo Chavez waiting to take the floor ; decided he could bully through with a backroom deal that he had crafted with a handful of countries . So , Copenhagen was a disaster , not just because countries were n't ready , although that 's the story that is generally told , but also because the Danish government completely screwed it up . And then Barak Obama showed up and decided to act like the gunslinger in the OK Corral saying , " Meet me out back , boys , and we 'll fix this . " The damage done to global negotiations by the Copenhagen Conference can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ negotiations before Copenhagen began in the mid 1990 's with the creation of the World Trade Organization ( WTO ) , which took away from environmental treaties the only useful enforcement mechanisms , which were trade sanctions . <p> One of the innovations in the post Copenhagen process was not to wait until COP-21 to find out what pledges countries were prepared to put on the table . The idea was for countries to bring forward their pledges in the first quarter of 2015 so that everyone would know what the INDCs ( Intended Nationally Determined Contributions ) were going to be ahead of time . So it was no surprise to anyone , or a critique of the conference 's success or failure that current pledges from all governments , if they are all achieved , total a projected 2.5 -- 3.7 degrees Celsius ( of temperature increase ) above preindustrial levels . This is why there is pressure on all countries to improve their targets . The targets are not embedded in the treaty so at any time a country can put in place a new one so long @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Now , back to the Paris negotiations : I saw the language that was negotiated last year in Lima , Peru ( Lima Climate Change Conference -- December 2014 ) weakened during the last negotiations ( before the Paris Summit opened ) of the final ADP ( Ad Hoc Planning ) Working Group which took place on October 19-23 , 2015 . The Canadian election took place on October 19 , 2015 and the Harper Government was still in the room trying to make the agreement less legally binding . It freaked me out entirely that even after our election the negotiating position of Canada did not change . <p> So , the context here is that it mattered to get some success out of Paris . It was essential to get a work plan to go forward with . In the language of the people who work on this , it mattered more to get the " architecture " right so that there would be a workable approach that would continue to hold governments to account and push us toward better targets . So , if this treaty @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it would be a disaster . But it 's not that . And no one there thought it was that . <p> So what we have now are two things that were agreed to in Paris . One is the Paris Agreement , which will be legally binding ( on all signatories ) as soon as 55 parties who produce over 55% of the global Greenhouse gas emissions ratify it . That will probably take effect by 2020 . If we are to keep climate change within 1.5 degrees Celsius everyone understands that if you wait until 2020 ( to ramp up the targets ) it will be too late . <p> Which is the second thing that was agreed to in Paris . It is called the COP Decision ( Decisions adopted by the Conference of the Parties ) and is a work plan in which the next important date is April 22 , 2016 , with a signing ceremony for the agreement to be held at a high level at the United Nations to push countries to improve their targets . The ( existing ) INDCs are n't good @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ targets -- asking , " Have the INDCs been improved over what they were in Paris ? " -- starts in March . <p> CGM : So that process has been constructed so as to maintain momentum and to have teeth ? <p> EEM : It does n't actually have teeth because there are no enforcement mechanisms such as trade sanctions . The " teeth " that there are , are in what 's called " global stock taking , " in the frequency of the reviews ( of the INDCs ) and , essentially , global peer pressure . <p> CGM : How will that global stocktaking be done ? <p> EEM : It will be done every five years . The first one is actually built into the COP Decision , although it 's ... called a " facilitative dialogue " in 2018 to assess all the ( greenhouse gas emissions ) targets . <p> If there was a mistake in ( regard to the ) the Kyoto Protocol , it was that it was agreed to in 1997 and the end point was in 2010-2012 , which meant @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ stocktaking . Now it will be done in 2018 , 2020 , 2025 , and 2030 . There is not a single government representative at COP-21who failed to realize that the totality of current commitments was twice too weak . The COP Decision gives us the tools to push forward for reducing emissions . The Paris Agreement by itself does n't save us . <p> I 'm also pleased that through the negotiations in Paris , at every step , the document got stronger , which is almost unheard of . What was that last decade of Canada all about ? Did we really descend to such depths of stupidity , vitriol , dysfunctionality , regressive politics , and sheer meanness ? The full cost of this reactionary lurch will probably never be known , but , dark clouds always having silver linings , two opportunities do present themselves : <p> 1 ) We can learn from how Canada descended into this chasm and make changes to make sure we never go down this road again . As the American election campaign illustrates , demagoguery can rear its head anytime and there will always be some people who are seduced by its primitive philosophy and mob mentality . In the last Canadian election the trial balloons of " barbaric cultural practices " were already aloft . Had the Harper Conservatives won another majority , who know where these would have touched down . <p> Citizens and governments work for decades to dispel hatred and intolerance , yet these genies can be conjured up by demagogues in an instant . And by urgent I do mean urgent . <p> Addressing climate change , ratcheting up our commitments , and making the changes required to meet them simply can not wait . Reforming an economy sinking from an over-reliance on raw natural resources extraction and primitive notions of economic development can not wait . The ever-increasing economic inequality that is needlessly crippling the aspirations of far too many Canadians is a blight whose resolution can not be postponed . The desperate circumstance of life on too many native reserves , struggling with poverty @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and substandard education , cries out for resolution -- not in future decades , but now . <p> Relying on knowledge , harnessing evidence-based reasoning , employing processes of consensus and compromise in our political conduct , placing social , environmental , and economic justice squarely in the limelight of political action , honouring our nation-to-nation commitments to indigenous peoples could move us a long way down the path that we need to go . <p> During Elizabeth May 's tenure on the Canadian environmental and political stage she has proffered many suggestions about where we should be going . As have many others . The opportunity that affords itself , now that it is 2016 and we can look at the era of the Harper Conservatives in the rear-view mirror , is to serious engage with these issues in a political climate that discards toxic hyper-partisanship and embraces informed , respectful dialogue . <p> Yogi Berra famously opined that , " If you do n't know where you are going , you will wind up somewhere else . " It 's time we heeded his advice .

THE CANADIAN PRESS/Jeff McIntosh <p> The pitch to renew the Conservative Party by embracing strong environmental policies faced hostility at the Manning Centre Conference , the major annual gathering of the country 's Conservative politicians and conservative thinkers . <p> " We 're playing in the wrong arena , " said an attendee after a panel on green conservatism at the conference in Ottawa Saturday . " We 're trying to win at a game we 're not going to win at . " <p> She was responding to a discussion on stage over how carbon pricing , despite the antipathy toward it from the federal Conservative party , could be marketed as a conservative idea . <p> A carbon price , which conservatives have pilloried because it would restrict growth in the oil and gas sector , is actually a right-wing policy because it puts the onus of environmental protection on markets , the argument went . <p> But the attendee , who @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the proposal was too cerebral and would n't make sense to average conservative voters . <p> " How do we as conservatives put a responsibility on individuals to make better choices instead of playing at this high level where there is obviously no consensus ? " she said . <p> But taking government out of the equation and putting power on individuals is precisely what a carbon price would do , said National Post columnist Andrew Coyne , one of the panelists . Once a price is introduced , its costs get passed on to those who use carbon , such as every person who drives a car or flies a plane , and they , the thinking goes , will gradually change their behaviour . <p> " Once you can get people over the hump of accepting that they 're going to have to pay a price for something that they 've previously got for free ... once you can get people over that large political hump ... then day-to-day , people are going to be doing it everyday without even thinking about it , " said Coyne . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ mainstream conservative opinion , most notably as Alberta 's oil sands industry sought new pipeline routes and global leaders tried to negotiate international emission limits . <p> That 's because certain powerful conservatives have chosen to enter the debate over climate change by criticizing the science , despite the overwhelming consensus among scientists behind it , said Christopher Ragan , economist at McGill University and another panelist . <p> When the science it that solid , " Do n't have the battle about whether we should protect the environment or have the battle over the science of climate change or other environmental issues , " said Ragan . " Have the battle about how to do it . " <p> Conservatives have largely become the spokespeople for market-based solutions in the political realm , so why not spread that strength to environmental solutions , he said . <p> " If we have a problem that we need to solve , you want to come up with the best , most conservative and consistent way to solve that problem and surely it is to used market-based measures , " said Ragan . @ @ @ @ @ @@7456063 <p> When Premier Kathleen Wynne said cap-and-trade will cost the average Ontario household approximately $156 more in energy costs starting next year , consisting of a $5 per month increase in the cost of home heating and a 4.3 cent per litre hike in the cost of gasoline , she forgot to mention something . <p> That is that those are n't the only commodities whose prices will increase because of cap-and-trade . <p> Even using the Liberals own figures demonstrates why their estimate of $156 annually is absurdly low . <p> The Liberals say their cap-and-trade program will bring in $1.9 billion annually in 2017 . <p> Given that there are 4.9 million households in Ontario according to the 2011 Canadian census , that means , according to the Liberals own numbers , cap-and-trade will cost the average Ontario family -- to start -- about $387 annually , 148% more than the Liberals claim . <p> Granted that 's a rough calculation and in fact , I asked the government in Thursday 's budget lockup what its estimate of the total costs of cap-and-trade to the average @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ $156 a year figure it offered only covers estimated increases in home energy costs . <p> I was told the government had done some calculations on this , but there were too many factors to give an accurate number at this point . <p> Be that as it may and contrary to the impression the Liberals have tried to create , cap-and-trade does n't just raise the price of gasoline and natural gas . <p> It raises the prices of all goods and services which use fossil fuel energy , meaning virtually all of them . <p> For example , when the price of gasoline rises under cap-and-trade it does n't just increase the cost of gas to families . <p> It also increases the cost of transporting virtually all goods to market , which is then reflected in higher consumer prices as businesses pass along their increased costs to the public . <p> The Liberals claim they will return to Ontarians a portion of the $1.9 billion a year cap-and-trade will cost them next year by , according to Finance Minister Charles Sousa 's budget speech , directing " resources @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ transition to a low-carbon economy . " <p> Such vague assurances are useless in calculating the true cost of cap-and-trade to Ontarians until we know what these initiatives are and how much money the government claims the public will be able to save through them . <p> Progressive Conservative MPP Lisa Thompson , the party 's environment and climate change critic , said that according to private sector estimates , cap-and-trade could cost the average Ontario household $400 more annually for gasoline and $475 more for natural gas heating alone . <p> Whatever the real costs , cap-and-trade has the potential to increase government revenues over time as it decreases the number of carbon credits available to industry , permitting them to burn fossil fuel energy which emits greenhouse gases linked to climate change . <p> As the supply of carbon credits decreases , the demand for them will increase . <p> When cap-and-trade starts next year , the Ontario government is estimating an initial carbon price of $17 to $18 per carbon credit , with each credit allowing an industry to emit one tonne of carbon dioxide or its equivalent @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ credits -- purchased through government auctions , which is how the government profits from cap-and-trade -- to consumers in the form of higher prices for goods and services . <p> But there 's no guarantee the Liberals ' carbon price will remain at $18 per tonne forever . <p> Depending on demand , Ontario 's carbon price could easily rise to , say , $30 per tonne in the near future , almost doubling the cost to the public , although an economic downturn would lower the cost of carbon credits . <p> Finally , cap-and-trade is the most expensive and least efficient and transparent way to lower greenhouse gas emissions . <p> A carbon tax which increases taxes on consumption ( in effect , a sales tax ) while returning all the money raised to the public in the form of income tax cuts , is the most transparent and effective way of reducing emissions . <p> Of course , it 's also revenue neutral , which is why the desperate-for-cash Wynne government has no interest in it . <h> Using the Wynne government 's own numbers , it 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ claim <p> @@7465297 <h> Canada in 2050 : land of climate-change extremes at current emissions levels <p> Steam rises as people look out on Lake Ontario in front of the skyline during extreme cold weather in Toronto on Saturday , February 13 , 2016 . Canada is a land of extremes , from car-freezing cold to crop-searing heat and drenching rains to drought . But you ai n't seen nothin ' yet . By 2050 ? ? ? within the life expectancy of most Canadians ? ? ? scientists say that if current emissions levels remain unchanged , climate change will be well established . THE CANADIAN PRESS/Mark Blinch <p> EDMONTON - Canada is a land of extremes , from car-freezing cold to crop-searing heat and drenching rains to drought . But you ai n't seen nothin ' yet . <p> By 2050 -- within the life expectancy of most Canadians -- scientists say that if current emissions levels remain unchanged , climate change will be well established . <p> It will be warmer : a cross-country summertime average of about two degrees . It will be wetter , mostly , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ may sound good to a country that describes summer as four months of poor sledding . And global warming will bring perks , such as the chance to grow different or more abundant crops . <p> But gentle averages , however , are not what Canadians will experience . Climate change will feed into Canada 's already considerable natural variability -- and not to smooth it out . <p> " The kind of changes one anticipates are more likelihood of drought or more likelihood of wet periods , " said Greg Flato , Environment Canada 's top climate modeller . <p> " If you think about temperature extremes , as the climate warms the likelihood of getting a very hot extreme becomes greater ; the likelihood of getting a very cold extreme becomes less likely . " <p> The extra rain , for example , is unlikely to fall in a gentle spring shower . Look for it in great flooding downpours or winter rains that drain before they can nourish crops . <p> John Pomeroy , a Canada research chair in water resources at the University of Saskatchewan , points @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ already declined by one-third on the Prairies . The number of multi-day rains has increased by half . <p> " Farmers need to adapt to that , to being inundated and flooded quite a bit , " he said . <p> Heat-loving crops like corn could become much more common in Canadian fields . <p> But water availability could limit the advantage of a longer growing season . Southern Canada 's modest precipitation gains are expected to be lost through higher temperatures . <p> Wildfire seasons already begin weeks before they used to . In the Northwest Territories , where temperatures are climbing faster than almost anywhere on earth , the 2014 fire season set a record of 3.4 million hectares of scorched forest . <p> Aspens , the most common leafy tree in the boreal forest , are dying at twice their historic norm , " part of a larger-scale pattern of climate-related dieback episodes , " says Natural Resources Canada . <p> By 2050 , look for big parts of the boreal forest 's southern fringe to be brand-new prairie . <p> " Drought-prone spruce will be lost first @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ replaced by some form of prairie grassland , " said a 2009 report from the Canadian Council of Forest Ministers . <p> Glaciers , an important source of water for western cities during low-snow years , are on their way out , said Mike Demuth of the Geological Survey of Canada . <p> " Glaciers in the Rocky Mountains are pretty much going the way of the dodo bird , " he said . <p> Nor do the changes stop with the land . <p> " We are already seeing ( fish ) species shifting their distributions , " said William Cheung of the University of British Columbia . " Warmer water species from the south are appearing in the north area and some of the ( northern ) species are suffering because the ocean becomes too hot for them . " <p> Look for overall catches off the Pacific coast to decline between four and 11 per cent by 2050 , Cheung said . Salmon hauls will drop between 17 and 29 per cent , herring by up to half . <p> There is , however , good news . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and manila clams . In the Atlantic , catches are expected to increase -- as long as fishers can sail further north . <p> " Smaller-scale fisheries may not be able to fish from their home ports a lot further , " Cheung said . <p> Commercial fisheries could also open in an ice-free Arctic Ocean , with turbot , Arctic cod and Arctic char . But much science is needed to determine if those fisheries are sustainable . <p> " It will be risky , " Cheung said . " Once fisheries have developed , it 's very difficult to scale it down . " <p> THE HIDDEN DRIVER <p> Canadians will also have to deal with climate change 's global impacts . <p> " It complicates the U.S.-Canada relationship , " said Rob Huebert from the Centre for Military and Strategic Studies at the University of Calgary . <p> " We know that in the southwest ( U.S. ) they 're pretty much getting maxed out in terms of available water sources . In a United States that is water-deprived , they 're automatically looking northward . " <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ situations , said Huebert . Refugees , fleeing expanding African deserts or the strife caused thereby , will be knocking on Canada 's door . <p> " What I 'm seeing is an acceleration in the collapse of some of these societies and it seems to be coming from the expansion of the desert , " Huebert said . " ( Climate change ) definitely seems to be an intervening variable that is exacerbating the situation . <p> " It 's the accelerator of what 's already a bad situation . " <p> Some worry current predictions are too conservative . <p> " All the work we 've done may be falling apart because nobody expected to see the Arctic exploding with heat , " said University of Lethbridge climate modeller James Byrne . <p> Even with this year 's El Nino , Byrne said the Arctic is falling apart faster than anyone thought it would . <p> January was the ninth straight month of record-breaking global warmth and saw the greatest departure from average of any on record , says NASA . It was hottest in the Arctic -- four @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to interpret at this point , " Byrne said . " How much faith can we really have in them ? I 'm worried about that . " <p> Most climatologists say the big impacts -- especially if the world does n't reduce its carbon emissions -- do n't even start until after 2050 . <p> Climate change forecasts , said Flato , only predict more uncertainty . <p> " We do n't know where that ultimate change will be , " he said . <p> " It 's a bit worrisome , not knowing what the future will be for my son or my grandchildren . The uncertainty of where it will get to is a worry to me . " <p> Note to readers : This is a corrected story . An earlier version said the amount of water falling as snow had declined by half . <h> This article has not yet been rated . <p> We want you to tell us what you think of our articles . @@7465311 <h> Nelson Wiseman : Externals crucial to Ontario budget <p> Ontario has tabled a budget that promises free tuition to many college and university students and tuition relief to most . The provincial Liberals also say they are well on their way to balancing the Ontario budget in 2017-2018 -- despite a formidable debt and increases in expenditures . <p> U of T News spoke with Nelson Wiseman of the department of political science about the **31;350;TOOLONG deficit , the implications of the tuition plan and the role governments play in determining -- and predicting -- the state of the economy . <p> What do you think of the Ontario budget ? Is this a good budget ? <p> I was surprised that the fiscal situation has improved as much as it has and that the deficit is going to be so much lower than originally projected . But on reflection , I realize that a lot of the improvement -- most of it -- has had nothing to do with anything the government did . When you ask whether it is a good budget , it 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ good or bad today ? " The weatherman does n't determine the weather . The deficit has shrunk because energy prices are a lot lower , because interest rates are so low , and because the decline in the value of the Canadian dollar allows exporters to fetch more money for their products . The government is paying less interest on the debt . The Ontario economy has benefited from low energy costs and a hot Toronto real estate market . None of this has anything to do with government policy . The government did sell 15 per cent of Hydro One , but that 's a one-time revenue benefit . In the future there will be less revenue from Hydro One . <p> The budget projects a $4.3-billion deficit for 2016-2017 . Have the Liberals done enough to reduce the deficit ? <p> It 's a small deficit compared to what they had projected a year ago . But they 're also making certain assumptions , including that the federal government will transfer more money to the province . It may , but there remains a huge debt , the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The danger is interest rates spiking up . Interest rates are determined in international money markets . With higher interest rates , Ontario would really be over a barrel . Right now , even with remarkably low interest rates , Ontario spends more paying interest on the debt than it does on higher education . And the expenditure on post-secondary education is quite large . <p> Can the budget be balanced by 2017-2018 ? <p> I take budget projections with a grain of salt . One of the things I 've learned is how dynamic and volatile factors are outside of the control of government . A government can control what it spends in certain areas . It can decide to give x amount of dollars to hospitals , to universities . It ca n't determine what the unemployment rate will be or how many people will be on welfare . And this is what determines whether welfare expenditures will go up or down . It ca n't determine whether corporations are going to make huge profits or sustain huge losses . If they make huge profits , then the government @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ projections is that the Ontario economy will grow by 2.2 per cent this year . Well it might . I think it will grow more than the Canadian economy . But it might not . What if we have a dramatic spike in oil prices or the value of Canadian dollar in the coming months ? None of that is within the control of the Ontario government . We also have equalization payments from the federal government . For the last few years , Ontario for the first time has been receiving equalization payments because its economy has n't been doing as well as the national economy . With Ontario doing much better now , the province may not get the boost in equalization payments next year that it got this year . <p> Is the government sacrificing significant funds to offer tuition grants for families earning under $50,000 ? <p> My takeaway is that there is n't any significant financial change for the government . They 've now got various programs for grants and bursaries , so they 're consolidating and simplifying . In terms of the cost to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ any more money , and if so , not much . The main thing they are doing is boosting families that make less than $50,000 a year . And I think that 's good . It 's not going to involve a significant boost of government money . It shuffles things around so students coming from families earning under $50,000 will benefit . In terms of the amount of money the government puts out in grants and loans and so on , it wo n't make much difference . <p> How will Ontarians react to an increase in gas prices to tackle climate change ? <p> I do n't think any tax increase is popular , but right now it 's easy to do . After it 's done , people forget about it in a few years . That happened in British Columbia , where there was outrage at the beginning . Here , there wo n't even be outrage because gas prices now are so low . <h> EVENTS <p> CSUS and F. Ross Johnson Distinguished Speaker Series presents ROBERT A. @@7466135 <p> Prime Minister Justin Trudeau will wax eloquent of course about how much he 's done about climate change already . <p> And premiers from " boast to boast " will claim they are doing a fantastic job with carbon taxes , cap and trade , and electric cars . <p> But Trudeau 's likely biggest contribution to climate change so far has been negative -- jetting around the world emitting untold amounts of greenhouse gases visiting six foreign countries . <p> The premiers , meanwhile , are far more concerned with the disastrous drop in world oil and gas prices than they are with climate change . <p> Natural resource exporting provinces like B.C. and Alberta have been pounded , while others suffer the consequences of a battered national economy . <p> But all being politicians , lots of lip service will be paid to carbon pricing , renewable energy and making polluters pay . Let the greenwashing begin . <p> However , they are privately praying harder than Pope Francis for China to start booming again and buy our oil , gas and anything else that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ And none need prayers answered more than Trudeau . He has gone from promising to balance the budget last April to a $30-billion deficit this year alone . <p> Trudeau 's failing credibility on budgets could undermine his climate commitments . <p> But no one takes a back seat to BC Liberal Premier Christy Clark on green hypocrisy . <p> While Clark claims " we can be the world 's clean energy superpower " the Canadian Wind Energy Association just blew out of B.C. due to her government 's disinterest in renewable energy . <p> Meanwhile , environmentalist prophet of doom author Naomi Klein believes , " Our economic system and our planetary system are now at war " -- so presumably capitalism must be recycled , consigned to the food scraps bin of history . <p> But " Workers of the world unite -- you have nothing to lose but your SUVs , flat screen <p> TVs and Xboxes ! " may not resonate . <p> So prepare for announcements of imminent armageddon from environmentalists and grandiose , unbelievable platitudes from politicians . <p> Neither truly address the harder reality @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ converted or predictable promises without practical plans . <p> Bill Tieleman is a former NDP strategist .
